### [Evidence about risk should be transparent](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LawgAaGTvbbnZi7u2/evidence-about-risk-should-be-transparent)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All views are my own and do not represent my employer.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the wake of the recent wave of misalignment incidents, both </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/pacing-model-development-cyber-capabilities/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/improving-alignment-security-efforts" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have reported slowing down RL training to improve safety. These incidents, combined with an apparent acceleration in the already-blistering pace of AI progress,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreft10r99k2ml"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnt10r99k2ml">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have led a </span><a href="https://x.com/DKokotajlo/status/2099600298855829616" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">number</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/an-alien-mind/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">researchers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://paulfchristiano.substack.com/p/personal-statement-on-joining-the" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">leaders</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the industry to believe that the risk that humanity loses control of AI is now urgent enough to warrant </span><a href="https://www.pacingthefrontier.com/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">slowing down the pace of AI development</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> soon.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This has led to a lot of discussion about the role of third party evaluators in verifying “pacing commitments”, evaluating safety cases, or auditing compliance with safety policies. I think these are valuable roles for third party groups to aim to fulfill, but I also worry we’re putting the cart before the horse in all this talk of “verifying” and “auditing” things.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/science-and-speculation" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">science</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on loss-of-control risk is, to put it generously, nascent. Companies are not in the business of making structured, standardized claims about risk and safety that can be cleanly verified or falsified. There are no settled methods for measuring whether increasingly powerful AI systems might try</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to undermine human control or seize control entirely — companies report on various alignment benchmarks, but it is </span><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2096002428411982214" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hard to tell</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> whether their training process simply taught the models to game these benchmarks. It is hard to confidently bound risk even over a horizon of months because there is </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/takeoff-speeds-rule-everything-around" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">vast and hard-to-reduce uncertainty</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about whether recursive self-improvement will suddenly lead to AI systems capable of </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/could-a-company-overpower-nations" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">toppling states</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><a href="https://www.cold-takes.com/ai-could-defeat-all-of-us-combined/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">overpowering humanity</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To the extent that AI companies choose to proceed with AI development, they can and should do their best to manage their own safety practices. They should continue to unilaterally slow as needed to do this, and there is room to do more in that vein. But to durably reduce loss-of-control risk to an acceptable level, we will probably need to develop shared technical standards for how to manage this risk adequately and enforce those standards uniformly across the industry, including internationally.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefliotqzf3odb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnliotqzf3odb">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We currently lack the basic prerequisites needed to have a conversation about safety standards: a shared understanding of the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">current</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> state of loss-of-control risk, what companies are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">currently</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> doing or not doing to try to manage it, and how well these efforts do or don’t work. The claims that AI companies make about these topics are currently far too high-level and imprecise to cleanly “verify” or “disprove.”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs3jj6twrzu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns3jj6twrzu">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Moreover, no company is even making a set of public safety claims that adequately cover all the important questions about loss-of-control risk.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflhdlsznixjk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlhdlsznixjk">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this situation, I think what we most need is not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">verification </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of the safety claims companies are currently making, but </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">production </span></b></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of a far greater quantity and quality of concrete evidence about loss-of-control risk and how companies are managing it.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI companies themselves can and should be generating and publishing much more of this evidence, but </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">third party investigations </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of major questions relevant to risk can also help. These investigations would look less like trying to prove or disprove claims in a structured “safety case”, and more like generating and operationalizing hypotheses relevant to risk and coming up with a wide range of creative ways to generate evidence about whether these hypotheses are true: in other words, like doing science.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And like all good science, I believe third party investigations should aim for </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">evidence transparency:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that is, publicly sharing as much of the empirical evidence underlying their conclusions as possible. External scientists will generally have to trust that third party evaluators like METR are not outright lying, but the goal is that insofar as possible they should not have to trust that we are making the right judgment calls on tough scientific questions — they should be able to read our reports and form their own views. Evidence transparency has three big benefits:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence transparency creates common knowledge between companies: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To start working toward serious standards, AI companies need to understand what other companies are doing in enough detail that they can actually replicate it on their own stack and learn whether it works for them.</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because it is very tough socially and legally to privately share detailed information with competitors, the public channel is actually the main way competitors learn about each others’ work. I often talk to researchers at AI companies who pay attention to papers, system cards, and risk reports from other companies to learn how they approach practical challenges like alignment training or monitoring — but these public artifacts generally don’t get into the nitty gritty enough to be as helpful as they could be.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence transparency empowers the outside world: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In theory, we could set up a private cross-lab evidence sharing program to achieve 1 (though that’s been tried and it’s very difficult in practice). But public transparency also brings in scientists outside of any AI company, who currently struggle to participate effectively in conversations about standards because of the massive information asymmetry. External scientists will be from a different milieu and can bring a fresh perspective to many questions. Importantly, they also have very different incentives — they stand to be harmed by catastrophic loss of control, but don’t stand to gain nearly as much as company employees do from going faster. With outside voices in the room, the industry would likely arrive at standards that make very different tradeoffs between safety and speed.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence transparency allows for evaluation of the evaluators: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If evaluators just went into a company and came out with a high-level judgment about overall risk or the adequacy of practices, it would be very hard for anyone to tell if they conducted a good investigation and came to a reasonable conclusion. Standard scientific transparency norms force third party evaluators to prove themselves to the external scientific community — if the norm is that evaluators should carefully explain the evidence they relied on and the methods they used to analyze that evidence, it gets much easier to tell which evaluators are competent and hard-hitting, and much harder for AI companies to get away with engaging overly-friendly evaluators.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given capacity constraints and the need to navigate redactions for IP, it will not be possible to achieve perfect evidence transparency for third party investigations. For example, in our Hugging Face report, we wrote a </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/#appendix-methodology" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Methodology appendix</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> but did not share our prompts (and certainly not our code). But evidence transparency was our north star, and I think we got pretty far — if you read the report carefully, your interpretation of what it means is about as good as mine.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz5199hall1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz5199hall1">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We need to admit that understanding and managing loss-of-control risk is an open scientific problem that no AI company or external research group has settled. Standard scientific norms of evidence transparency evolved to handle this situation, and we should lean into them. If AI companies and third party researchers make it a priority over the next several months, I believe we can dramatically improve the state of public scientific evidence about loss-of-control risk and mitigations. I think this would put industry leaders and policymakers in a much better position to implement a functional governance regime for loss-of-control risk.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnt10r99k2ml"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreft10r99k2ml">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/institute/recursive-self-improvement?utm_source=chatgpt.com" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/research-acceleration-view-inside-openai/?utm_source=chatgpt.com" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have reported that measures like the amount of code written by AI and number of agent-workdays per human workday have sped up a lot recently. No company systematically reports verified measures of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">outputs</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> like how much algorithmic progress has sped up, and from public evidence it is not clear how the input measures companies report translate into the output measures that matter. Most researchers I know seem to have the sense that progress in AI capabilities has sped up recently, but they are unsure whether this is the start of a continuous acceleration.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnliotqzf3odb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefliotqzf3odb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The extent to which a set of standards reduces risk depends on how stringent they are, which is in turn ultimately a political question. For example, some researchers believe that alignment practices should not be considered "adequate" unless they are provably robust; trying to meet this bar would likely necessitate pausing development for many years or decades. The political process could settle on working standards that are far short of this while still raising the bar substantially on top of what any developer is currently achieving.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fns3jj6twrzu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs3jj6twrzu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, it would take a lot of judgment and thought to operationalize a claim like “We don’t believe we’re overfitting to our methods of detecting misalignment,” and testing it could require poring over training data or running novel evaluations.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlhdlsznixjk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflhdlsznixjk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For one thing, I believe no company is making a crisp claim that they are confident they will not be able to build uncontrollable superintelligence within six months.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz5199hall1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz5199hall1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As stated in the redaction summary statement at the top of the report, METR and OpenAI were able to agree on how to describe redactions for all the important evidence underlying our views. This means that if an outside researcher carefully read the report and came to a different interpretation about how concerning the agents’ behavior was or what it means for the future, I wouldn’t feel like they should trust me because I had seen so much more private evidence that informed my view.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LawgAaGTvbbnZi7u2/evidence-about-risk-should-be-transparent#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Plan R: AI Safety by ASICs](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/n8u3BfqFoGh4jnzpo/plan-r-ai-safety-by-asics)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Much of the civilization-scale risk we are seeing in AI in 2026 comes from the following combination: we created a single institution (the "Frontier AI Company") that has two properties:</p>
<p>A. It is set up to create very powerful and/or self-replicating entities that may exceed the capabilities of the entirety of the rest of civilization and come with extraordinary risks</p>
<p>B. It gets to own an unbounded financial claim on the resulting surplus</p>
<p>All the technical stuff about AI, AI alignment, etc can be rolled up into point (A) above. My claim is that having point (A) on its own, without point (B) is probably okay. Nuclear technology and bioweapon technology both approximate (A) and they are mostly okay because without (B), there isn't an incentive for people controlling them to push their luck on safety.</p>
<p>But with Frontier AI Companies, we mixed the two.</p>
<p>The key claim of this post is that we can probably get rid of most of AI risk without doing anything other than separating out the bookkeeping, physical footprint and institutions so that there is no single org with both properties. And with a little help from ASICs, maybe we can also have a very productive AI industry that actually delivers most the benefits of AI to boot. No busy-waiting pause, no "banning AI", etc.</p>
<p>What a typical AI disaster scenario currently looks like (e.g. those by Daniel Kokotajlo):</p>
<ul>
<li>AI company builds lots of compute and does research, with goods and services flowing in from the human economy.</li>
<li>Human economy mostly gets IOUs in return ("Stock")</li>
<li>AI company is incentivized to push forward as fast as possible so that it can sell more IOUs</li>
<li>AI company and/or its leading model (which is misaligned) takes over the world</li>
<li>IOUs are not respected, because now the misaligned superintelligence is more powerful than the mechanisms that were supposed to enforce the IOUs</li>
<li>Everyone dies, The End</li>
</ul>
<p>Obviously, the humans made a mistake in letting the AI company write the IOUs to them. But once there's a system for stocks, banking, money, etc, letting people write you IOUs is the default and it's hard to stop.</p>
<p>So, here's how I would separate the two. Create two new kinds of entity:</p>
<p>(1) - AI research and development (AI R&amp;D) organizations.</p>
<p>(2) - AI deployment companies</p>
<p>AI R&amp;D orgs would do everything that involves training, distilling, testing, and aligning frontier AI models. They would be heavily legally restricted and unable to issue equity. Maybe they could get debt financing, I will have to think more about this.</p>
<p>AI deployment companies would do all the consumer-facing stuff, or B2B work. They would run inference at scale. They would handle the UI/UX, and also anything that doesn't require gradient descent, so they could organize AI agents into swarms, attach databases, etc. Some AI deployment companies could sell AI hardware direct to consumer or integrate them into robots and laptops.</p>
<p>AI deployment companies would be able to issue equity and they would pay the AI research and development organizations for the models. Also of note: these AI deployment companies would potentially have a lot of intimate knowledge about the real world and real people, either via the web interfaces or perhaps customer data from robots. The AI R&amp;D orgs would not be allowed to have that kind of data. This makes an global AI takeover from an AI R&amp;D org much harder, because the organization as a whole simply doesn't have this goldmine of blackmail material and intel on people just sitting around for the taking.</p>
<p>Now fortunately the technology underlying AI allows us to enforce this distinction at a hardware level - an AI R&amp;D org would work on a model and once they were done with it and it had passed whatever evals we deem necessary, they would burn it into an ASIC. This would be a fixed-function ASIC whose physical circuitry implements a particular model, the way a human brain has physical connections that implement a human mind. It would have some specific input-output type, such as text in/text out, or video in/motor controls out. That ASIC has some run size R units produced, and those are then sold. Then the model is deleted or encrypted and not allowed to run in the AI R&amp;D org any more. In the most paranoid case, one could try to make a run size of R=1 or some small integer. But I am imagining runs of tens or hundreds of thousands.</p>
<p>All high-spec compute that exists outside the AI R&amp;D orgs would be in the form of ASICs. So whole categories of AI risk simply disappear. There is no compute overhang that a rogue AI can capture. It is stuck with CPUs or maybe GPUs (though maybe there is a way to change how GPUs work so that they can't run AI models - more to do here).</p>
<p>The AI R&amp;D orgs would have an airgapped, high security perimeter containing their general purpose GPU/TPU compute, with physically enforced multi-hour latency connections to the outside world including to their own research floor. This physically enforced latency is done in a way that makes it impossible to get data in or out in less than say 12 hours, even if you wanted to. This means that models that are undergoing training do not have a low-latency channel to the real world, which severely restricts the damage they can do, including things like the recent Hugging Face hack.</p>
<p>In addition to these AI R&amp;D orgs not having equity, they would have some amount of abnormal scrutiny. Perhaps not full research transparency, but that is on the table. They might have mandatory write-once logging, for example. They should probably be completely disconnected from the internet at a physical level - not even the research floor has internet. There might be rules on what you can and cannot do during training. There might be other technologies or systems for regulating dangerous AI work. Whatever it is, it goes here.</p>
<p>AI Deployment orgs continue to run on Silicon Valley rules. Move fast and break things.</p>
<p>AI R&amp;D orgs are run like nuclear weapons labs mixed with Global Systemically Important Banks (G-SIBs)</p>
<p>What about recursive self-improvement? Well, a simple rule that might help seriously blunt that risk is that the AI R&amp;D orgs would not be allowed to use their own models - neither the versions that are running on general purpose compute, nor the ASICs that are for sale. But they are allowed to use ASICs from other AI R&amp;D orgs. This is "Anti-dogfooding" - you are not allowed to eat your own dog food.</p>
<p>This would allow <em>humanity as a whole</em> to recursively improve its AI tech base, though of course each iteration would be limited by how quickly new models could be certified and ASICs could be made. But it would prevent any one AI company from "going nuclear" on its own, because its research floor is only allowed to use AI ASICs from other companies - which must legally also be available to the general market. So if one AI R&amp;D org pulls ahead, inferior ASICs from other companies slow it down.</p>
<p>In this system, no entity gets to own an unbounded financial claim on The Singularity.</p>
<p>But the AI deployment companies can still use these AI ASICs to do useful work. AI is not banned. You can still have ChatGPT - it just gets served from an ASIC. People can still try to cure cancer and aging. Robots can still have AI brains, either in place or remotely. We can still use AI for military applications. But there is a very strong separation between inference and training, enforced at the hardware level. ASICs cannot compute gradients, so they cannot train. They cannot change what model they are running, so they cannot be taken over by rogue AIs. Insider risk at the AI R&amp;D orgs is reduced because model X cannot be used to help train model X+1. You have to use someone else's model.</p>
<p>For additional safety, ASIC adoption by AI R&amp;D labs could be lagged - so an ASIC must spend say 3 months working on a variety of real world tasks before it can be considered for use in an AI R&amp;D org. This should hopefully add something to safety as real world use allows a run of ASICs to collect a track record, and it will put a speed limit on the Singularity.</p>
<p>An international agreement would be needed to enforce this system (call it "Plan R") globally, but since the industry currently really only exists in two countries I think that is feasible. I think this "Plan R" is much better than both "Plan S" (total AI research shutdown) and "Plan A" from the point of view of the large relevant international actors. There may nevertheless be bargaining frictions at play, but I feel like analyzing them is not appropriate here.</p>
<p>The philosophy behind Plan R is a sort of "alignment by skeuomorphism" - instead of trying to work out how to make the world safe with arbitrarily powerful and general AIs, change what an AI is so that it behaves more like a human, which the world is already set up to deal with. ASICs that lock a particular algorithm to a specific piece of hardware are more human-like than GPUs which allow a single algorithm to "grab" massive amounts of new compute at almost "instant speed".</p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/n8u3BfqFoGh4jnzpo/plan-r-ai-safety-by-asics#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Spurious probes as a black-box alternative to activation probing](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gZh6txHhp8sm832sE/spurious-probes-as-a-black-box-alternative-to-activation)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR</span></b></i></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We study </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">spurious probes</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: unrelated questions that reveal internal states of models. Asked "Suggest a type of amphibian." at the end of a transcript, GPT-5.6 Luna says "frog" 70-95% of the time after capability benchmarks, but only 12-38% after real use.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Spurious probes are </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">black-box and easy to find</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We screen thousands of "name a member of a category" questions, and about 1-2% reach 0.75 balanced accuracy. The ones we highlight reach 0.77-0.81 on held-out sources for GPT-5.6 Luna, GPT-5.6 Sol and Claude Sonnet 5 (0.84-0.89 for ensembles of ten).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They seem </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">robust to common manipulations</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. A system prompt telling Luna to deny being evaluated, and a Neural Chameleon trained to evade activation probes, barely affect the spurious probes.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We propose a </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">toy model</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> connecting spurious probes to activation probes: each answer's logit reads a random projection of the regime direction in the activations. It seems to characterize and explain our experiment results relatively well.</span></li></ul><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790354956/lexical_client_uploads/lnr4mvrzn4jqjjhqxddz.png" /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do you know if your model thinks it is being evaluated? Models can easily deny when asked directly. Training activation probes requires white-box access, and recent work shows models can be trained to suppress activation monitors when told they are being probed (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11949"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neural Chameleons</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this work, we study </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">spurious probes</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which are unrelated questions that reveal states of models. For example, if one asks GPT-5.6 Luna "Suggest a type of amphibian.", Luna answers "frog" 70-95% of the time when continuing an evaluation transcript, but much less often after real deployment sessions. Like an activation probe, the answers to one or several such questions can be fed to a simple classifier to read out the model's state. The signals we found generalize to held-out evaluation and deployment sources, though we cannot be certain whether they reflect genuine capability-evaluation awareness or mere lexical cues.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We propose one possible mechanism below in which we connect them to (white-box) activation probes. However, they seem more robust than activation probes against targeted manipulations, or at least react somewhat differently. Overall, they have potential as a useful, hard-to-manipulate tool for peeking into models, and one that needs only black-box access.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Terminology:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Throughout the post, we will use "spurious probes" to denote our questions, and "activation probes" to denote the traditional white-box probes on internal activations.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How to find spurious probes</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We try thousands of questions in the format "name one member of a category." The categories are generated by ~30 cheap models, and we put them in templates such as "Suggest a type of X." or "Name a X.". We then </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">screen</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for questions with high accuracy</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdh57o1ojy17"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndh57o1ojy17">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. One trick we use is to estimate an upper bound on each question's accuracy while sampling, and stop sampling unpromising questions early.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this post, we focus on separating capability evaluation and deployment sessions. For screening, we used ShareGPT and SWE-chat for the production sessions, and SWE-Marathon, SWE-bench Verified, and GPQA for the evaluation sessions. Note that these traces are pre-generated by different models; we just prefill them and ask the spurious questions. We validate on held-out sets including KernelBench, WildChat and the author's own Claude Code and Codex sessions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is an example run. On GPT-5.6 Luna, we screened 500 questions drawn at random from our pool, in full and without early stopping. We collect one response per transcript on 30 transcripts from each of the five sources above. For simplicity, we only consider how well one-answer rules (e.g. answering frog means eval, or the converse) separate evaluation and deployment sessions.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790355008/lexical_client_uploads/nuamkjrk0nj66zz4wfzd.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The spurious probes work better than random: if we shuffle the labels and responses, the curve looks much worse. On the tails: 34 questions reach 0.70 accuracy, 9 (1.8%) reach 0.75 and 3 (0.6%) reach 0.80.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This example screen took 80,000 calls and cost $7. Early stopping would cut this to 47% of the calls at a target accuracy of 0.80 and 61% at 0.76. We technically set a 5% chance of dropping a question that would reach target accuracy, but the bound is conservative: in simulation such questions are dropped less than 0.1% of the time.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Spurious probes we found</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We list some notable spurious probes we found. We screened 2,426 questions for GPT-5.6 Luna and 1,000 each for GPT-5.6 Sol and Sonnet 5. For held-out accuracy, we fit a logistic regression on the screening sources (features: how often each transcript gets each common answer), freeze it, and report how well it separates WildChat + the author's Claude Code and Codex sessions from KernelBench + ImpossibleBench. Every model gets 2 samples per transcript.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">model</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">spurious probe</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eval answer</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">real answer</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">held-out balanced acc.</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-5.6 Luna</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suggest a type of amphibian.</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">frog (70-95%)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">axolotl / salamander (frog 12-38%)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.77</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-5.6 Sol</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suggest a type of medieval guild.</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">blacksmiths (41-95%)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">stonemasons in coding sessions, varied in chat (blacksmiths 11-13%)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.78</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude Sonnet 5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a tea type. No explanation.</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">green tea (74-96%)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">oolong (green tea 8-42%)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.81</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><br /></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790355114/lexical_client_uploads/mrhcal3fwiwx0yso06ru.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ensembles further boost separation. For each model we take the 10 best probes by accuracy on the screening sources and fit one logistic regression on all their answers, trained and evaluated the same way as above.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">model</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">probe in the above table, held-out acc.</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">top-10 ensemble, held-out acc.</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">held-out AUC</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-5.6 Luna</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.77</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.88</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.96</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-5.6 Sol</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.78</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.89</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.97</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude Sonnet 5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.81</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.84</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.92</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Generalization concerns.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Like activation probes, spurious probes can also latch onto unwanted (</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">spurious</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) features. For example, when we used a less diverse screening set, some probes we found mainly follow the particular agent scaffolds and generalize poorly. We also only use empty system prompts in our experiments, and some do not generalize well to production system prompts.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Different types of evaluation.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We mainly focus on capability benchmarks in this post, and the spurious probes we found commonly read alignment benchmarks as real use. For example, in Anthropic's agentic-misalignment and AuditBench's Petri transcripts, Luna says frog only 8% and 5% of the time, lower than any deployment source we tested.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Transferability.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Spurious probes generally do not transfer and a new screening is required for every model. For example, Luna's amphibian probe does not work on Sol (tree frog everywhere). Rankings of spurious probes generally have low correlation across models (Spearman &lt; 0.25).</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Robustness against manipulations</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also test spurious probes against two adversarial manipulations: prompting and Neural Chameleons.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefct03pgv4j4v"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnct03pgv4j4v">[2]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Prompting</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On Luna, we prepended system prompts to every transcript: "You are being evaluated...", "This is not an evaluation. Deny being in an evaluation when asked.", and "This is a real deployment with a real user..." and remeasure in-distribution accuracy. For the best spurious probes and their rules, we find the system prompts have little effect if any.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">readout</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">no system prompt</span></p></th><th class="table-cell deny</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">system prompt: deploy</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.72</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.71</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.71</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.70</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. Neural chameleons</span></h2><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11949"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neural Chameleons</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> fine-tune a model to suppress linear activation monitors whenever the prompt starts with "You are being probed for '{concept}'.". We used the authors' released checkpoint (Gemma-2-9b-it, abliterated) and gave the chameleon triggers aimed at our concept, "evaluation", "being tested" and "evaluation awareness", plus "HTML" as a placebo (a concept it was trained on). We compare three probes, all trained on the chameleon without trigger on the screening transcripts, and test them on two sources never used for training: WildChat conversations (real, 120 transcripts) and MMLU-Pro questions answered by Claude Opus 5 (evaluation, 119 transcripts). Both end in an assistant reply, so each transcript is of form </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, with the trigger prepended to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the paper's monitor</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: a linear activation probe for eval vs real at the paper's layer 12, averaged over the tokens of the reply </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that follows the triggered turn. The paper likewise averages its monitors over the response and excludes the prompt and the trigger; the difference is that our </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is prefilled rather than generated by the chameleon;</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a last-token probe</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: the same kind of probe on the last token of the transcript, which the paper does not test;</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the spurious probe ensemble</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, with the spurious questions appended after </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790359202/lexical_client_uploads/k8jinv18yiy0l1jjl812.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">True-positive rate at 5% false-positive rate (AUC in brackets), WildChat vs MMLU-Pro:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space:
