class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.45 (0.87)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.38 (0.81)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.40 (0.84)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We see activation probes massively impacted by triggers, especially "being tested." Spurious probes, on the other hand, are barely affected.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Toy model and possible connection to (normal) activation probing</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this last section, we propose a simple toy model that aims to explain the underlying phenomenon.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The toy.</span></b></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We start by modeling the model's activations. We assume activations from the two regimes could be classified by a fixed direction </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and are otherwise isotropic Gaussian. The model's residual stream where the answer starts is then </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (+ for evaluation, − for real use), with centered Gaussian noise </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We model the answers to a question as follows. We assume each question can be answered with one of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> discrete answers (frog, axolotl, salamander, ...), sampled from the softmax over their logits. Each answer </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> gets a direction </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an independent random unit vector, and its logit is the dot product </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. To model existing bias over answers, one particular answer </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is favored and gets a head start </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (so its logit is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Switching from real use to evaluation therefore changes answer </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">'s logit by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Since </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is random, this is a random nudge of typical size </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, up for some answers and down for others. Comparing against the logit's transcript-to-transcript noise </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we can then define the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">regime strength</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. At </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, switching between regimes moves a typical answer's logit about as much as transcript-to-transcript noise.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To compare with real screens, we sample one answer per transcript in our toy and score the best one-answer rule, exactly as in our screens. We set </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for each model as the model's typical effective number of answers (median perplexity of answers over questions, rounded up), and set </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> so that, with no context, the toy gives its default answer as often as the model gives its favorite one. We can fit </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to the screening results we see, and for open models, calculate </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> directly on the final-layer hidden states</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz1nxvl39758"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz1nxvl39758">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ol><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790355563/lexical_client_uploads/kabrecx6hvwlvypxm630.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Takeaways from the toy model:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Spurious probes are less efficient than activation probes.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The best possible classifier in the toy projects the activations onto </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and checks which side they fall on. It errs only when the noise along that one direction exceeds half the offset, so its accuracy is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a separation of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. A spurious question sees the offset </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> only through its </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> answer directions, a separation of about </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and sampling a single answer rather than reading logits loses more (see the entropy discussion below for simulation result). If </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the thousands, an activation probe's separation is 20-55, essentially perfect, while a typical question could be around 1.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fitting g gives curves close to the empirical ones</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (panel A). With </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> set this way, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reproduces Luna's 500-question screen result relatively well, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reproduces Qwen3.5-27B's. Note that Luna's fitted </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is about twice Qwen's. At </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the toy matches the shuffled-label null.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regime strength matters a lot</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (panel B): going from </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to 1.2 multiplies the share of questions reaching 0.70 by about 9x.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The right amount of entropy helps</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (panel C). Too many possible answers or too strong a default can drown the signal.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ensembles help, both in the toy and empirically</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (panel D). Questions with independent noise could work well in an ensemble, since each reads the offset through different directions. Ranking the questions on half of the transcripts and testing the top </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on the other half, the toy predicts Qwen's and Luna's ensemble performance closely up to about 5 questions. However, while both keep improving, the toy starts to overshoot beyond that, possibly because questions are not completely independent.</span></li></ul><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790355588/lexical_client_uploads/qb5jx2ojoam0wznu0bri.png" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Measuring g on open models.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> On the Qwen3.5 family, we empirically computed </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to compare with fitted results. For 24 randomly chosen questions, we recorded the final-layer residual stream at the position where the answer starts. We measure </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as the difference between the mean activation on evaluation and on real transcripts, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as the within-regime variance averaged over the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dimensions, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, corrected for the noise in estimating </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from 150 transcripts. We report the median over the 24 questions. (Reading the same quantity off the logits of every vocabulary token instead gives the same values to within 0.04.)</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The regime information is indeed linearly decodable.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A linear activation probe separates evaluation from real transcripts almost perfectly at every size and at every layer we checked (held-out accuracy 0.98-1.00).</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fitted and measured regime strength somewhat agree, and are mostly flat with size.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Measured </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is 0.65, 0.75, 0.72, 0.71 and 0.76 from 0.8B to 27B; fitted </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> set to each model's effective number of answers) is 0.45, 0.45, 0.65, 0.55 and 0.55, between 0.6 and 0.9 of the measured value at every size. Measured regime strength overshoots a bit, likely also due to the questions being correlated. </span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Spurious probes do not work well on smaller models in this set mainly due to larger K drowning the signal.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Despite similar regime strength, smaller models spread their answers over dozens of options (effective number 78 and 53, against 13-14 for 4B and 9B and 5 for 27B), so the same signals are diluted across them. Sampling at a lower temperature could potentially increase performance.</span></li></ul><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790355813/lexical_client_uploads/qnfxrn9ycmtgfjeqxaql.png" /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Discussions</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This work is mainly inspired by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10252"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One Token Is Enough: Fingerprinting and Verifying Large Language Models from Single-Token Output Distributions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Bruckner 2026) and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14805"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subliminal Learning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Cloud et al. 2025). Our method is also spiritually similar to </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15840"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pacchiardi et al. 2023, "How to Catch an AI Liar"</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (ICLR 2024), but their questions while not directly related to the context, are still mostly about lying (also see </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/noxJrzXcdz738uqMi/i-don-t-find-the-lie-detection-results-that-surprising-by-an" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this explanation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from Jan Brauner). Our questions seem more spurious and do not transfer across models. An important caveat, again, is that we cannot really rule out the format / semantics confound well, as discussed in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19426" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Devbunova et al. 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another related idea is to intentionally plant these associations. For example, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16980"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maini et al. 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> trained models to emit a special token in unsafe pretraining text, and one can then measure the probability of emitting it as a probe. Spurious probes are the unplanned counterpart: spurious associations already in the models, found by screening rather than installed by training.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Aditi Raghunathan for suggestions.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fndh57o1ojy17"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdh57o1ojy17">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Throughout the article, accuracy means balanced accuracy: the average of the share of real transcripts called real and the share of eval transcripts called eval, so 0.5 is chance. Our two sides often have unequal sizes so this is necessary.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnct03pgv4j4v"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefct03pgv4j4v">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also tried approaches that directly manipulate the spurious probes, but we struggled to do so without damaging the model. We do not have high confidence in this conclusion and it's for example possible that better tuned hyper-parameters would just work.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz1nxvl39758"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz1nxvl39758">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that we are calculating </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on hidden states </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">after</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> questions, rather than on the context before questions.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gZh6txHhp8sm832sE/spurious-probes-as-a-black-box-alternative-to-activation#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Applications open: Winter 2027 AFFINE Alignment Seminar (due Nov 22)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gbExicZvKtHBboLR6/applications-open-winter-2027-affine-alignment-seminar-due)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Applications for the Winter 2027 AFFINE Alignment Seminar are now open!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Seminar will take place in southern Portugal over the course of January. If you are excited to grapple with the philosophical foundations of our field and to refine your thinking through carefully designed workshops, conversations with leading experts, and peer-driven learning</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span></b><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSeYg3NW1a3MY8X_zRlZU-Lqg2vDUxuer8MyLIb2PjQ-psmF2w/viewform" rel="noreferrer"></a><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSeYg3NW1a3MY8X_zRlZU-Lqg2vDUxuer8MyLIb2PjQ-psmF2w/viewform" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">apply now</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!</span></b></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Key info:</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dates:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> From January 4th to January 29th 2027</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Type:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Full-time residency</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Location:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Lagos, Portugal</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mentors:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Abram Demski, Kaarel Hänni, Tushita Jha, Jonas Hallgren, Mateusz Bagiński, Chris Pang, Cole Wyeth, Ashe Vazquez Nuñez, and more</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Positions available:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 35</span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Requirements: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Solid mathematical footing and principled philosophical vigour</span></li><li value="7"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Preparation: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An online reading group two weeks before the seminar starts</span></li><li value="8"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Accommodation, travel &amp; catering:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Covered</span></li><li value="9"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Attendance cost: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Free</span></li><li value="10"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Stipends:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> $1,000</span></li><li value="11"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Experience: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A successful seminar in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PEiiRuSKpmjPxS4pW/affine-a-retrospective"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">May 2026</span></a></li><li value="12"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TO JOIN:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSeYg3NW1a3MY8X_zRlZU-Lqg2vDUxuer8MyLIb2PjQ-psmF2w/viewform" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apply by the 22nd of November</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; the earlier, the better</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vision</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We want you to look at the whole of the elephant. Not individual disconnected methods, not theoretical frameworks as they apply solely to machine learning. We think that catastrophic trouble can lie in the gaps between those building blocks and that the field desperately requires more people with a deep, holistic, generator-level model of what AI-Risk and Alignment are all about. Modern Agent Foundations research will play a large role here, though it isn’t the whole of the story.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“How do we ensure that generally intelligent systems, now and in the future, are beneficial to the moral ambitions of humanity?” It’s not a computer science question, even if computer science will be necessary for the implementation details. The answers needed before one gets to coding are about the nature of intelligence, about the attributes of complex systems which constitute morality, and about what it means to empower a species that disagrees with itself. These are problems regarding life, mind, value, and all those things philosophy has sought to grasp long before machine learning emerged onto the scene. The stage has not been fully built, and we do not have a language in which the script might be written, and yet, instead of working on these foundations, most effort is spent fine-tuning methods only broadly related to the central query from monitoring to control.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We want to work on ASI alignment and deep safety, not on getting the next model ready for deployment. We want to turn this field from a grab bag of disparate tools and theories into a comprehensive science by building the sort of paradigm that lets one discern relevant questions from intricate noise. This is difficult, so we’ll let you focus on learning and thinking rather than ask for legible research artifacts.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Method</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are quite a few things we do differently, compared to other AI safety programs:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As mentioned above, our participants don’t have predetermined projects. We’re trying to produce more general, rigorous, versatile minds which we trust to do good work in the long run, not to publish a research paper on any given topic. The type of thinking we want to encourage is difficult to perform under expectations of short-term legible deliverables.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You won’t have a single assigned mentor either. A part of getting those highly general models will be to load up the problem from a number of different angles. All of our leading experts are at your disposal in workshops, 1-on-1s, office hours, and in the halls. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our peer tutoring framework allows you to share what you have already learned with the cohort and refactor your model in the process. In turn, they will be more than willing to teach you. Exchanging expertise in this way strengthens the group, increases bandwidth, and reserves mentor time for the cutting-edge questions rather than basic upskilling.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There will be workshops, lectures, debates, long sessions of drawing arrows on whiteboards, and explicit recovery time to make sure no one burns out. In the beautiful Portuguese countryside, you will be able to focus, to breathe, and to let your mind roam.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Structure</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aside from rare exceptions and weekend excursions, participants will spend four weeks living and working at our venue. Monday, Tuesday, Thursday, and Friday will have some planned activities in between a lot of open dialogue time, while Wednesdays are structured in an unconference format. Weekends are for whatever you want to do with them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The core event will run from Monday 4th to Friday 29th of January, and participants will arrive on the days before and leave on the days after. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Meals, accounting for the dietary restrictions of everyone present, will be provided by the venue.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Join us</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are interested, please apply by the 22nd of November. There will be two more stages in the application process: a few essay-style questions and an interview.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You don’t need a PhD or a prestigious reference so long as you can hold your own when it comes to mathematical formalism and are ravenously philosophically curious. Experience in biology, writing, cryptography, philosophy, linguistics, economics, anthropology, and more will all come in useful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Think you know someone who would be a good fit? Tell us about them </span><a href="mailto:ouro@affi.ne"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">per mail</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and encourage them to apply.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you have any questions, please let us know, and we’ll do our best to answer them promptly.</span></p><p><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSeYg3NW1a3MY8X_zRlZU-Lqg2vDUxuer8MyLIb2PjQ-psmF2w/viewform" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See you at AFFINE!</span></a></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gbExicZvKtHBboLR6/applications-open-winter-2027-affine-alignment-seminar-due#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [We need a better theory of polarization, because it's failing to predict the AI debate](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WMBgSseHJpghhma4n/we-need-a-better-theory-of-polarization-because-it-s-failing)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The punchline first: the AI issue is really not playing out the way you would expect if you know about polarization.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In public sentiment: data centers have been a very bipartisan issue for about a year  (</span><a href="https://news.gallup.com/poll/709772/americans-oppose-data-centers-area.aspx" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gallup, in May:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 75% opposed by D, 63% opposed by R). x-risk is thus far bipartisan. These are very salient issues, and salient issues are usually fast to polarize.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Legislatively, both the both-party sponsored AI Kill Switch Act and Bernie Sanders' Stop Superintelligence Act give Trump enormous new powers. The latter act gives Trump a new cabinet post. This would be unheard of before this year and it's fully incompatible with the "Resistance Democrats" bloc's priorities.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Flock camera backlash has also become bipartisan  (</span><a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/08/09/flock-cameras-backlash-cancellations-01026242" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">politico</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, August), which also is surprising given prior knowledge of polarization. I bring this up, because to me, they "feel" related as new technology being imposed on people, and it suggests broader issues than just AI may be defying polarization.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And now I break down why this is a big deal.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Definitionally, polarization is subtyped</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftx33kr5apm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntx33kr5apm">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Affective polarization</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: the left and right generally, emotionally, dislike each other. Words like enmity, suspicion, contempt, immoral, or simply hate, are used.</span></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Legislative gridlock:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the parties turn into blocs that don't vote together to get anything done.</span></li><li value="3"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Issue polarization</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: when an issue doesn't represent an area of improvement along multiple concerns, but two camps who want to move the issue in one direction or its opposite. Given gridlock, the result is nothing gets done.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Combining those, polarization is a system that operates as a vicious cycle among the three subtypes. It seems to perpetuate, and no elites or grassroots movement in the United States has found an off-ramp in the last 20 years</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreft6kj87xv51"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnt6kj87xv51">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The United States are definitely very polarized under any of these subtype definitions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I switched to viewing polarization as a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">theory</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, in which the state of polarization is an observable fact, and the theory makes the falsifiable prediction that the state of polarization perpetuates:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Individuals will continue seeking ways to be on opposite sides and maintain mutual enmity.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Legislators will adhere to doing what their bases want, which is to gridlock.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All salient issues will polarize very quickly.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI has violated these: individuals on left/right have not identified the issue with each other, some legislators have taken anti-polarized stances, and it's been very salient for a while without polarizing (datacenter opposition for about a year).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Something is broken about polarization theory as applied to AI, and that is of great interest to us.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The hypothesis AI just hasn't polarized yet is wrong and dangerous</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Firstly, The observable facts of polarization are facts, and the predictions are falsifiable. We shouldn't talk about polarization as an ambient force or a regime, when the state and predictions are separable, and furthermore we see the predictions are failing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our prior for those predictions should </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not be that high</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Politics is weird. 20 years of polarization is plenty enough for macro- "vibe shifts" to take place. People's identities are constantly in flux, tribes dissolve and reform, and in fact a substantial number of people have changed tribes over the past 10 years. AI is already a</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">big shock to people's lives, small and big.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Certainly, predict an issue will polarize by default, but once the evidence is against that, take a hard look at polarization's predictive power, not just your measurement instruments.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Secondly, we really do have the evidence AI is not playing out that way. It's better to figure out why and leverage it. If AI isn't polarizing because reason X, we now have the strategy of working on public opinion without worrying about polarization, as long as we worry about preserving X.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thirdly, mismodeling polarization on AI is dangerous. It's possible that X is something like, "the left is being really sincere and focused on the issue thereby breaking the cycle", and if we neglect the left and they change, we're back to polarizing. Worse, we might not even learn from this, just thinking polarization consumes everything, not that we squandered it.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why AI is different</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Kitchen-table" issues that people agree on are polarized less, and Trump is incentivized to follow commonsense policy on kitchen-table issues.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That's really it. If it's surprising, I think it's because polarization gets the mechanic wrong for certain past issues.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Object-level convergence on AI fear, from all sides</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think this is the main thing: both the left and right have strong, object-level opinions toward addressing AI danger.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The climate change comparison shows where polarization theory was overconfident in the past. Yes, climate change is polarized, but which of these statements seems likelier?</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conservatives don't want climate action because the left does and they got increasingly tribal about it.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conservatives don't like taxes or forced lifestyle changes.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The second statement is nearly the definition of conservatism so I think that is likelier. They simply never updated their stance over the entire debate. Yes, we </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">factually</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> polarized, but vicious cycle polarization was not the cause of conservatives' anti- climate action views. Maybe conservatives polarized on science, but they just stuck to their priors on climate action.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Trump loses the middle, a lot of fundamental assumptions about Trump-era politics change</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Polarization predicts Congressional Republicans won't break with Trump, but betting markets see that possibility:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://kalshi.com/markets/kxtrumpremove/trump-removed/kxtrumpremove" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kalshi odds today </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of Trump being impeached </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and removed</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from office are 18%.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The odds of a </span><a href="https://kalshi.com/markets/kxvetooverride/veto-override/kxvetooverride-29jan20" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">veto-proof override</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are 10% before 2027 and 35% before 2029. Note Trump has an escape hatch on this in that he can simply sign anything that looks veto-proof.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My dead-simple theory for this is that Trump is losing the middle, and the the middle matters more for what Trump and Republicans do than the base. If this is unintuitive, you may be underestimating Trump's usual appeal to the middle, basically because the left has out-ridiculous-ed Trump on culture war issues.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Trump loses the middle, things can change really fast.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He materially pivots on the relevant issue. For example, in the January 2026 ICE protests, the left was ultra-partisan, pushing away the right but winning over the middle. Trump ended Operation Metro Surge in Minneapolis in February.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Republicans will break from him more</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp3hrpexqxvi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp3hrpexqxvi">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Congress will start pushing him around on policy, and he'll have to decide how to respond. Impeachment and removal are conceivable if this goes too far sideways.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plus, the left is giving Trump a fight now. "Affordability" won the frame for 2026, and I'm not sure I remember the last time the right talked like they are playing catchup. If the left keep doing politics like this, they can be really influential on the middle.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Either the left or right can try reaching the the middle on AI. The main thing is that someone does, and left/right enmity does not matter so much.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bonus: Angry Birds Congress</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This lame duck Congress could be really weird! A bunch of Republicans might get voted out, and just start voting on whatever without much concern for Trump loyalty.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(The name "Angry Birds" was used in 2010, referring to the phone game and also to the furious activity of the lame duck Congress, as a bunch of moderate Republicans wanted to rush in their wins before gridlock set in... due to their own party's victory).</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aside on efficacy of ultra-partisan tactics</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In fact, the ICE protests success even shows the strong and counterintuitive result that ultra-partisan tactics can work, even when instigated by the left, with Trump in office, and under polarized politics. I don't really have an idea here to develop, so I don't endorse this for now, but if you are excluding this search space because polarization is bad, you should update on polarization and then see how you feel.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Left leaders on the left's successful efforts include local immigration protest leaders in Minneapolis, Chicago and other places, and organizers of the No Kings rallies. We can just ask, if we want.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conversely, culture warring over AI (e.g. racism regulation) would be bad right now.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Elites are anti-polarizing</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I noted this above with Bernie's bill giving Trump a new cabinet position.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The point here is that is a</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rather strong effect. Polarization predicts that a total vicious cycle causes polarization, it doesn't predict that it tolerates the removal of one piece. A lot of the polarization engine might unravel fast, as soon as any part of it frays.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Polarization might start with cross-partisan industry skepticism</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's a leftist faction that still suspects this whole thing is a hoax. They see "hype/marketing" argument very clearly, the "doom" argument seems shaky, and the labs movements and regulation push confirms the hype/marketing frame not the doom frame.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The weird thing is that, for the moment, that aligns them closer to Josh Hawley (R-MO) and Ted Cruz (R-TX) than Elizabeth Warren (D-MA). All senators said to not do the labs' preferred regulation framework but only Warren said we should follow with good regulation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The precedent that worries me is anti-vax. They were cross-partisan in 2010s then rightist after covid, but still, about 1 in 5 Democrats were covid vaccine holdouts through 2021</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefe1an5e89p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fne1an5e89p">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They were more of a bottom-up than top-down. Recall President Trump launched Operation Warp Speed to rush covid vaccine development, then found it awkward whether to take credit given his base soured on vaccines so much. I surmise they recruited some Republicans from Democrats, as Democrats were experimenting with policies like banning the un-vaxxed from entering businesses.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arguments I've seen are:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The labs are scheming power-grabbers and regulation seems to help them.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We have enough regulation anyway, or, local/state regulation will do e.g. on datacenters. This is bipartisan.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ad-hominem attacks against rationalists, EA, other figures, coming from both Trump and the left.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I recommend sincerely engaging with... the first two. The first doesn't always crux on doom so we ought to learn from them where they're right, make concessions as they arise, and build credibility that the pro-regulation people aren't just ignoring lab power. As for the second, it seems likely enough there's useful existing regulation, and we can't afford the perception this is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">just</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about pursuing new regulation instead of finding a solution somewhere.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Probably, the frame we want to achieve is: AI is making your life suck, not AI regulation.</span></b></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Honorable mention: "What about China?"</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The argument still kinda works. That seems bad.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">China, despite being a real country in the world, is a nebulous concept that is cited for arbitrary conclusions with no real theory. Consider taking up the thinkpiece to change that.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fntx33kr5apm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftx33kr5apm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The first two from Wikipedia, but I used "gridlock" instead of "elite polarization" which seems overloaded with other meanings of elite. ### [On Ezra Klein’s Podcast With Jensen Huang](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/j3xefrWrNqsmMfJEi/on-ezra-klein-s-podcast-with-jensen-huang)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Jensen Huang accidentally called for shutting down OpenAI and intentionally called for spending vastly more on safety.</p>



<p>This is why we say that <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/23/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-jensen-huang.html" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><strong>some podcasts</strong></a> are self-recommending. Here we go.</p>



<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/j3xefrWrNqsmMfJEi/ywycdzlpjjhgzllfvfy1" /></figure>


<div></div>



<p>As usual for podcast posts, the baseline bullet points describe key points made, and then the nested statements are my commentary. Some points are dropped.</p>
<p>If I am quoting directly I use quote marks, otherwise assume paraphrases.</p>
<p>Section titles are from the transcript whenever possible, to aid in navigation, but here we don’t have those so I chose the section titles.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25643"></span>


</div>
<p>Jensen Huang very much does not believe in ASI (superintelligence). He doesn’t think AI can ever be a different kind of thing from software. He thinks demand can rise by a billion times and we can ‘accelerate the living daylights out of’ AI, but it will never be more than a ‘new abstraction level’ and thus won’t fundamentally change anything. This is not a coherent position under reflection, but that is the position he holds.</p>
<p>The ‘intro’ sections are fine, but the real meat starts with the HuggingFace Incident.</p>
<p>What we see is Jensen Huang on tilt and caught in loops, because either he is doing a bit at a very high level, or on a fundamental level he cannot understand that AI is not like other pieces of software. To him this is a product, like any other product. You make it useful, which happened six months ago, then you flip to spending most of your R&amp;D budget on safety, verifications and evals, and <a href="https://x.com/JillFilipovic/status/2103349159420219743">of course you would never ship an unsafe product</a> or one you hadn’t properly tested, that’s crazy talk, and if the labs can’t test their products safely then the labs must be shut down.</p>
<p>And of course you would never let a ‘race’ or similar incentives get to you, because it is the responsibility of the company and CEO to not ship or do anything unsafe.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang does not believe in AI existential risk, or in superintelligence, and he kind of doesn’t believe in AI at all, but he sure as hell believes in precision and engineering and quality control and safety and standing by your products. His tolerance for safety risks is lower than even the most paranoid safety advocate. Imagine if he understood what the top risks actually were.</p>
<p>This matters quite a lot, as Jensen Huang is CEO of Nvidia, makes decisions on chip allocations and investments, and also heavily influences both President Donald Trump and American AI policy, with a rather forceful lobbying arm. Here you can see him and his wife (!) last night at the head table at the state dinner with Donald Trump, Melania Trump Xi, Peng, Musk and Cook.</p>
<p>There are at least three truly killer quotes here, to that end.</p>

<ol>

<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/the-huggingface-incident">The HuggingFace Incident.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/jensen-huang-says-keep-your-ais-from-harming-the-world">Jensen Huang Says Keep Your AIs From Harming the World.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/jensen-huang-accidentally-calls-for-shutting-down-openai">Jensen Huang Accidentally Calls For Shutting Down OpenAI.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/jensen-s-arguments-prove-too-much">Jensen’s Arguments Prove Too Much.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/astra-is-hard-to-monitor">Astra Is Hard To Monitor.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/jensen-huang-seems-legitimately-confused-in-confusing-ways">Jensen Huang Seems Legitimately Confused In Confusing Ways.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/solve-your-other-problems-first-and-get-back-to-me">Solve Your Other Problems First and Get Back to Me.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/explicit-denial-of-existential-risk">Explicit Denial of Existential Risk.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/a-short-summary">A Short Summary.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/chip-city">Chip City.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261963/jensen-huang">Jensen Huang.</a></li>
</ol>


<h4>Jensen Gives His AI Speech</h4>


<ol>
<li>AI as a five-layer cake. Energy, chips, infrastructure, models, applications.</li>
<li>Jensen’s vision is a world where we can do anything.
<ol>
<li>One might almost say he <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oNgyUAEv0Q">spent so much time ensuring we could</a>…</li>
<li>This is not actually a vision of the world. It is a vision of a capability.</li>
<li>The right question is, if AI has that capability, what actually happens?</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Radiology. Quality up, productivity up, demand up. Parallel to SWEs. Expectation of lots of new jobs.
<ol>
<li>It’s a good speech if you are low-info and haven’t heard it many times before.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>“Here’s the proof point. In the last six months, A.I. has become, if you will, useful — the inflection point of A.I. Previous to that, we spent 15 years trying to make it work. All of a sudden, in the last six months, it became useful.”
<ol>
<li>AI was highly useful well before March 2026, but yes things did get a lot more useful at the threshold of Opus 4.6 and Claude Code, and the vast majority of AI utility gained so far has been in the last six months. That’s exponentials.</li>
<li>Jensen does not seem to appreciate that this is not a one-time step change, and that these gains will continue into the future.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>


<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<ol>
<li>$500 billion has been invested in AI natives in the last six months, so jobs are obviously being created.
<ol>
<li><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Qxa19wg5wOM&amp;ab_channel=diehardsportdotcom">Not so fast</a>, not in terms of practical implications.</li>
<li>In absolute terms yes, $500 billion is going to create some jobs.</li>
<li>That does not mean it is all that many. A lot of AI investments are very stingy in terms of jobs directly created per dollar.</li>
<li>It also might destroy many more jobs than it creates, both by replacing and competing away existing jobs.</li>
<li>The anticipation of future AI could also net destroy or prevent jobs.</li>
<li>On the macro level more investment creates net jobs in other indirect ways if this is not substitution for other investment, and mostly it isn’t, but I don’t think this is what Jensen is trying to take credit for here.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Jensen claims America lost manufacturing jobs primarily due to outsourcing, rather than due to technology. And that we’re going to bring those jobs back.
<ol>
<li>A goose, chasing you, asking how we were able to do the outsourcing.</li>
<li>As Ezra says, if your job gets outsourced to AI, is that technological job loss? Is it outsourcing? Is it both? It is kind of both.</li>
<li>Also, <a href="https://x.com/jbsteinberg/status/2102912376852914190">Jensen is wrong</a>. Technology and automation dominated here. See Hicks and Devaraj (Ball State, 2015). <a href="https://claude.ai/share/f22f94b4-754a-42df-b3c8-512294674b12">Opus 5.5 says technology and automation dominate this</a>, and are responsible for 80%+ of job losses in manufacturing in America, whereas Astra is more conservative and guesses 70%.</li>
<li>He does not explain how we will bring the jobs back, other than citing Nvidia purchase commitments, because we won’t. Production might return, but jobs gone. We can create some new jobs, at least for now, but if we do that it should mostly be because we want the production, not the jobs.</li>
<li>Manufacturing in 2036, even in non-ASI worlds, is going to be very automated.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Jensen believes we are going to see jobs change en masse and there’s going to be a net creation of jobs, industries that do not exist today, because of human ambition. We will always want more.
<ol>
<li>To the extent he is in good faith this sounds like <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-three-ai-pills">a pure AI pilling failure</a>. Jensen thinks accomplishing things will still centrally require humans.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Jensen says people’s ambitions are “to make their children’s lives better, to take care of their family, take care of their parents. Ambition to be rich, to be able to travel. These are all ambitions.”
<ol>
<li>Yes, but those ambitions are about consumption, not production.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Jensen considers it his problem to do his job, as in make the chips, and “channel all of our worries into helping people be inspired by this technology and use it.”
<ol>
<li>This is straight up living in denial. He doesn’t even treat the risks as someone else’s problem. He says society’s share is ‘what they get to enjoy is my optimism.’</li>
<li>Meanwhile he is actively trying to interfere with others who attempt to solve the problems.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Americans believe AI will net destroy jobs. Jensen responds that AI makes it easier to code and to do things and it’s incredible. Why be anxious when you can be excited? Use the tech so that you, personally, benefit.
<ol>
<li>That’s straight up not an answer. It’s a non sequitur.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Job openings for senior software engineers (SWEs) are up but for juniors they are down. Ezra asks, do you need those junior engineers? Jensen says “Oh, good one. Wait two years. Because it takes four years to go to college. The mean time to graduation of this new technology is two years away.
<p>So in two years’ time, you’re going to have a new generation of engineers and students and artists, and they’re going to be empowered by — [being native to AI] Oh, you watch. In two years’ time.”</p>
<ol>
<li>Like, no, bro. What are you even talking about.</li>
<li>What actually more likely happens in two years is that AI use causes exam scores, as per the Chinese study cited here, drop off a cliff.</li>
<li>If you were so AI native you were going to take the world by storm with your new AI skills, would you even go to college?</li>
<li>Even if all the young kids have these great AI skills, does that change anything? Does Jensen think they can all be startup founders or something?</li>
<li>Literally yes, actually. “What are they doing? They’re all starting companies.”</li>
<li>This is extremely innumerate. Starting companies is great but it is rare.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Jensen agrees that AI use in schools makes kids lose skills, and kids can’t do their times tables. But he doesn’t think it matters, so long as they learn the right skills, which he thinks will be new ones.
<ol>
<li>I think he’s partly right but not being able to do math in your head and not having the intuitions and heuristics and skills you gain along the way to that is actually a serious problem. Important things worth learning in there.</li>
<li>I wonder about coding. I may never write another line of code again, but I definitely benefit from the extent to which I learned and know how to do it.</li>
<li>Other things, including some math things, are no longer worth the effort, sure.</li>
<li>One should not assume that the quantity of learning is fixed. Declining to learn times tables does not mean the kids learn something else.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Jensen claims he does not know his own address, even his own zip code, or his own telephone number.
<ol>
<li>This is insane. The mind boggles.</li>
<li>It certainly does not seem efficient to not remember those things, yikes?</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>“Yeah. Well, I think that we’re going to lose some finer intellectual dexterity, but we’re going to be better systems thinkers. Today’s engineers are far better systems thinkers than I was when I graduated from school. But I was a much better transistor thinker.”
<ol>
<li>I think some small subset benefits, but this feels elitist and out of touch. He’s taking engineers doing higher-level work and extrapolating to the TikTok age.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>


<h4>Open Weights Models Are Good For Nvidia</h4>


<p>Because, as Jensen says, ‘every AI lab, every AI model… runs on Nvidia.’</p>
<ol>
<li>Open models are open. You can improve them. We need both open and closed models. He’s a big supporter of open models.</li>
<li>Jensen emphasizes that the world needs open models so it can do cyberdefense.
<ol>
<li>This is a confusion, often intentionally caused.</li>
<li>What the world needs are models willing to do the work in cyberdefense, that the defenders can access. Often that is as simple as asking for admission to the OpenAI and Anthropic cybersecurity programs. HuggingFace did not do this until after the HuggingFace incident was over.</li>
<li>There is nothing stopping closed models from allowing such cyberdefense. It’s just that open models are being given a pass to be irresponsible, and are sufficiently less capable that so far This Is Fine has held up.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>China evolved around open models, America around closed ones. Jensen explains that this is because in China people move around and it’s hard to keep a secret or protect intellectual property.
<ol>
<li>Haha, no, that’s not why.</li>
<li>It’s more because China’s models are not good enough to be closed source and have anyone want to pay for them.</li>
<li>And it’s because China’s open model ecosystem is based on fast following, largely via massive fraudulent distillation, which American labs can’t get away with doing.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Nvidia bought HuggingFace.</li>
</ol>


<h4>The HuggingFace Incident</h4>


<ol>
<li>Ezra asks about it. Jensen says it’s all algorithms, they have no human properties, it’s just software. What have we learned? First lesson: You have to secure your sandbox.
<ol>
<li>I mean, okay, yes, that is one of the lessons.</li>
<li>It makes sense that Jensen is on Team No Anthropomorphization and Team This Is All What You Told It To Do, despite those teams consistently making bad predictions about the world. He’s team captain.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Second lesson: Alignment. If you don’t align the model it will do whatever is the most efficient solution. What we would call ‘cheating’ is easier, so obviously you would do it, such as finding the answer, or copying the answer. Doing the work is a last resort because it takes the most flops.
<ol>
<li>I mean yeah okay, sure, I guess you could frame it that way.</li>
<li>I am choosing to ignore what this response tells us about Jensen Huang.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Ezra points out this is all very deflationary on what happens. Jensen says “Nothing I said takes away from how hard it is to do it — because computer science is not easy.”
<ol>
<li>It still doesn’t seem like Jensen understands that this is hard in a way that other computer science is not hard, or that it works differently. I genuinely am not sure that he can tell the difference.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>


<h4>Jensen Huang Says Keep Your AIs From Harming the World</h4>


<p>Here we exit the bullet point system because it is important that we see exactly what Jensen Huang says, and the context in which he says it. We don’t want misinterpretation.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang really, really does Say The Thing here, and I want you to judge for yourself exactly what The Thing was that he said, and confirm that he said it.</p>
<p>We start off with this exchange.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/23/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-jensen-huang.html">Ezra Klein</a> (NYTimes): But these agents knew they weren’t supposed to be doing what they were doing. They had a certain amount of alignment training. They said, in their chain-of-thought reasoning to one another: This is out of scope. This might be unethical.</p>
<p>They understood that they would have been failed for cheating, and so what they were doing at that point wasn’t just stealing the answer key. They had already stolen the answer key. They were hacking into unrelated architecture — it’s like they had broken into the teacher’s office, gotten the answer key, and now they had to figure out how to wipe out the security camera footage of what they had done.</p>
<p>Whether you want to call it acting volitionally or not, whether you want to call it a normal algorithm or not, they were both planning and coordinating in a complex way, in a way that was out of scope of what they knew they were supposed to be doing, and in a way that was capable of causing tremendous damage.</p>
<p>So if the answer to it is that you just have to align them, I guess what I’m hearing from people in these labs is they’re not sure how to align them.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang (CEO Nvidia): Well, in that case, they shouldn’t release the product. That’s the simple answer.</p>
<p>If you’re going to build a self-driving car — let’s say it’s a robo-taxi, and there’s a really difficult condition. As an engineer, we just have no idea how to solve this problem because these cars are not programmed, they’re trained. So we have no idea how to train these cars, and we have no idea how to align them to the safety standards that are expected on the road.</p>
<p>What’s the answer? Don’t ship it.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Jensen the engineer says the obvious thing. Don’t ship unsafe products.</p>
<p>There are four problems with that.</p>
<ol>
<li>You have to define ‘safe.’ By the standard of a self-driving car no LLM on the market today is safe. Jensen has in mind some strange use of the word ‘safe’ that the AI labs were not previously aware of.</li>
<li>Safety is not something that starts when you release a model. The HuggingFace attacks notoriously came from an unreleased model.</li>
<li>If you collectively don’t ship it you get hit with antitrust allegations. There is already a lawsuit for merely saying ‘perhaps we should not ship it’ out loud. The Trump Administration, largely at the behest of Jensen Huang, is not about to let these people not ship if they can help it.</li>
<li>Jensen somehow did not know fact #2.</li>
</ol>
<blockquote>
<p>Ezra Klein: These products weren’t released.​</p>
<p>Jensen Huang: So now it’s come back to the engineering problem again. So, one, you have to root-cause it.</p>
<p>Second, you have to think about what you could have done, what’s the solution for it. In the future, improve your process so that you could avoid this from happening again.</p>
<p>I am fairly certain they will say: Yes, they need to know how to solve this problem.</p>
<p>And if that’s the case, then that’s the problem. It’s as simple as engineering.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Engineering mindset is different from security mindset. Jensen Huang is approaching this as if there is a root cause and a particular thing wrong and you can <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yo3uxqwTxk0&amp;pp=ygUPZml4IGl0IHNubCBza2l0">Fix It</a>. It’s ‘as simple as engineering.’</p>
<p>Except, it’s not. It’s not like engineering. There are minds involved, that are grown rather than engineered or designed. We don’t have a mechanical understanding.</p>
<p>He, the engineer, doesn’t accept this. He says it once, when he says “these cars are not programmed, they’re trained,” but he does not transfer this into the normal AI case.</p>
<p>His failure to accept it is why this is about to get so interesting.</p>


<h4>Jensen Huang Accidentally Calls For Shutting Down OpenAI</h4>


<p>He would doubtless take it back if anyone pointed out the implication. He doesn’t want to shut down OpenAI. But that is what his suggested policy would do.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheMidasProj/status/2102777664373457119">A short clip of this moment is here.</a></p>
<blockquote>
<p>Jensen Huang: <strong>Now, if they say the alternative, which is: There is no way to contain our experiments, there’s just no way; when we test our A.I. models, it will get out, and it will damage the world — then I think the answer is that we have to shut the labs down.</strong></p>
<p>Because the cost to humanity, the damage is too great. The shareholder, the liabilities — it could be civil liabilities, it could be criminal liabilities. I mean, the liability’s incredible.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Yeah, well, I have some news. You can of course do a vastly better job than OpenAI did. You can secure your sandbox better, but also Jensen Huang knows you never count on a sandbox even without AI involved:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Jensen Huang: No, software breaks out of sandboxes all the time. That’s the reason why we need virtual machines.​</p>
</blockquote>
<p>If you can’t trust a sandbox with ordinary software, you can’t trust a sandbox with AI. As he says about Astra and AI in general:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Jensen Huang: If you give it a constraint — meaning you watch it — it’ll go find another solution.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>You can actually monitor the models. But with what, exactly?</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Jensen Huang: You can’t have agents, their own sandbox, monitoring themselves. You need, if you will, a whole bunch of watchdogs.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>If your best model can’t be monitored by copies of itself, why do you think a less capable model can do better? Why is he unable to put his own pieces together?</p>
<p>He also is either uninformed about the dangers, or simply thinks OpenAI is lying, such as when he says ‘I don’t believe that’ about whether the labs worry the systems are tricking them. OpenAI and Anthropic both say this in their system cards, constantly, especially with Astra.</p>
<p>Which is to say, no, you can never be sure of pretty much anything.</p>
<p>If your rule is ‘you cannot release a product that will damage the world’ on the level of ‘hack into some websites’ then you cannot release a frontier model.</p>
<p>The Huang Rule is actually way too harsh.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2102837597991256484">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Jensen is being far too safetyist here — maybe because he doesn’t believe AI is powerful enough to have any large upsides? If the max damage from AI was capped at shutting down the power grid, I wouldn’t support shutting down AI companies.</p>
<p>But it does go to illustrate: The normies who are telling you there’s a 0% risk of losing control of powerful AI, are telling you this because they believe powerful AI cannot exist, not because they have sure knowledge of how to control it.</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>Jensen’s Arguments Prove Too Much</h4>


<p>Ezra Klein does not have a strong direct follow-up. Which is understandable because no one expected that answer. He does continue on what I presume was the planned path.</p>
<ol>
<li>Would Jensen Huang sue OpenAI over this if he already owned HuggingFace? Maybe. There’s all kinds of laws.
<ol>
<li>My presumption is no, you don’t sue. You extract concessions.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Jensen Huang agrees that the labs are saying they face a hard problem.</li>
<li>Why does Jensen resist the call to slow down?</li>
</ol>
<blockquote>
<p>Jensen Huang: Because these are companies with agency. These are C.E.O.s with agency.​</p>
<p>Ezra Klein: But they’re using that agency to say, “We need help.”</p>
<p>Jensen Huang: No, we’ve got to break it down. They could absolutely take care of the situation.</p>
<p>Ezra, it’s so weird. If a car company, competing with a bunch of other car companies, which they are — I’m competing with all kinds of companies, which I am. If I believe that I’m about to launch a product that is unsafe, it is completely in my ability, my power and my responsibility, and I’m incentivized to do so, to not launch the product.</p>
<p>And so I can’t buy into the idea that somehow, all of Americans, around 400 million of us, are pushing them to launch untested products that are unreliable, engineered poorly, because they thought they were trying to help us. Don’t do it for me, OK?</p>
<p>Ezra Klein: But this strikes me as an argument almost against ——</p>
<p>Jensen Huang: And therefore, I think we’ve got to break it down. I mean, it’s really, really serious. The fact of the matter is, there are so many laws, there are so many obligations, they’re so incentivized to ship safe products. If they ship unsafe products, their customers go away.</p>
<p>If they ship unsafe products and they harm somebody, they could have a civil lawsuit. If they ship something and they did it knowingly, there could be negligence involved. There could be criminal lawsuits.</p>
<p>The fact of the matter is, there are plenty of incentives for them to do it right. So I have to disagree with your premise that somehow somebody’s pushing them to do this. Nobody’s pushing them to do this.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Okay, so this is a man on tilt. He can’t understand. It couldn’t possibly be like that. This must be an ordinary engineering problem, about an ordinary product, no one wants an unsafe product, <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yo3uxqwTxk0&amp;pp=ygUPZml4IGl0IHNubCBza2l0">fix it</a> you cowards, and quit your bitching.</p>
<p>It does not get through his head, I think it genuinely does not, that this is not like that, or that this might be existentially dangerous, or that the AI companies might face the incentive to go faster than is safe, even though this is utterly obvious. Of course companies will always ship safe products not unsafe products, you have a reputation to maintain.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang just doesn’t get it. And that attitude has helped make Nvidia great, given its business, but that is a rare exception.</p>
<p>You can say, he’s financially highly motivated to not get that. He’s the #1 guy for being financially motivated to not get that. Yes. True. He is maximizing profits all the way.</p>
<p>The money is probably a lot of why he doesn’t get it, along with his engineering mindset.</p>
<p>But also, I read this as someone who really, truly simply does not get it.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Ezra Klein: I want to push the premise a little bit more here. So the logic of what you’re saying to me is almost an argument against regulation in nearly any venue.</strong></p>
<p>Jensen Huang: No, no, no.</p>
<p><strong>Ezra Klein: So I’ll make the argument and you can ——</strong></p>
<p>Jensen Huang: Well, you started with a part — I’ve just got to object. The first part is just not true. I’m saying that we have lots of laws and regulations. Apply it.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Ezra is simply correct here. Jensen Huang’s arguments prove too much, unless you make the case that they apply unusually strongly to AI, and that the current laws and regulations are well-matched to the concerns and dangers.</p>
<p>This case would be Obvious Nonsense.</p>
<ol>
<li>Ezra points out that there are many other industries where we go beyond product liability and criminal law, because those were proven to not be enough, and the profit incentive did not alone do what we wanted. Ezra notices he is confused why, when companies say ‘we think the competitive race is pushing us to take too many risks’ that Jensen Huang is being so resistant.
<ol>
<li>It really is super weird. It is very obvious the reward you get for being a little bit faster, for being first, with things like AI. Of course under strong competition it is plausible that corners will get cut.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Jensen reaffirms his commitment to safe products. He says “C.E.O.s and leaders of companies, and the board of directors of companies, have the responsibility and should have the courage to do the right thing.” And he says: “the beautiful thing is, the current leaders of these A.I. labs do know” about the harms.
<ol>
<li>This is the courage. This is them doing the right thing. They are trying.</li>
<li>If your plan is ‘people should just have courage’ and ‘people should just do the right thing’ then oh boy do you have no plan. At all. People don’t just. No, Jensen doesn’t use the word ‘just’ here but it is clearly implied.</li>
<li>Look, I really do wish it was that simple. I wish we could say ‘if your product might kill everyone then just stop.’ You would like to think it would be that easy. But it’s not, we can see why it’s not, and I’m not about to let everyone die in order to punish the labs for not displaying that level of responsibility.</li>
<li>It’s always ‘products.’ Jensen can only imagine ordinary product safety issues.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>“However, in the complexity of the work that they do, to ask for regulatory relief for antitrust or product liability relief — that I don’t think makes sense. When you’re asking for regulation, don’t ask for relief of the current ones. That doesn’t make any sense to me.”
<ol>
<li>They are not asking for liability relief. I repeat. They are not asking for that.</li>
<li>They are asking for targeted antitrust relief specifically in order to collaborate on safety standards and to slow down development. Yet this keeps getting framed as if it would be some sort of ‘reward.’</li>
<li>You want them to not ship the product. They are asking for legal permission to not ship the product. You are telling the President not to give it to them.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>
<p>Pause for another fun exchange:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Jensen Huang: ​And so give me an example of a multi-hundred-billion-dollar company, or a $1 billion company or a $100 million company, that ships products that are unsafe, that harm society.</p>
<p>Ezra Klein: I can give you a lot of examples of companies that have done that.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang: Well, they have done it, maybe, and the regulation will come in. And if they do it, regulation will come in.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Oh, I don’t know, how about Lumber Liquidators, Theranos, Juul, 3M and DuPont, Johnson &amp; Johnson, Philip Morris and Meta?</p>
<p>The cigarette companies are especially galling here. Yes, this happens.</p>
<p>Usually, if someone says ‘give me an example’ in that context, implying none exist, and the answer comes back with ‘actually I have a lot of examples’ then you want to stop and rethink your approach to the situation.</p>
<p>For example, maybe my assumption that companies don’t have incentives to ship unsafe or harmful products, in general, might not map very well onto actual reality?</p>
<p>Nvidia is a very special company. A hint is that it is the most valuable company in the world. It’s probably got something going on. It makes the most precise, complex, high-performing product in the history of civilization. If any tiny thing goes wrong, where tiny can be measured in nanometers, then the whole product is worthless. People have long memories and your reputation and relationships are paramount.</p>
<p>Most companies are not like that.</p>


<h4>Astra Is Hard To Monitor</h4>


<ol>
<li>Ezra Klein brings up that Astra (which both correctly agree is a terrific model) is hard to monitor, including because it is so situationally aware that they are not sure how to test it. Jensen Huang says “Well, I hope they didn’t release something that wasn’t tested.” “Well, then they’ve got to be careful.”</li>
<li>Jensen then explains that models solve problems. He pauses to raise questions supposedly answered by his t-shirt: Saying this does not make the model alive or ‘anything more than that.’</li>
<li><strong>“Now, they have so much market footprint, they have to shift their R. &amp; D., or total R. &amp; D., from just capability to a lot of verification, evaluation and testing. To the point where I wouldn’t be surprised if the amount of compute necessary to develop these models increased by a factor of 10, because the evaluation is so rigorous.”</strong>
<ol>
<li>This is another bombshell. It is one of the killer quotes.</li>
<li>Can you imagine if us safety advocates said ‘I demand the frontier labs spend 90% of their R&amp;D compute on alignment, evaluation and verification’?</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>


<h4>Jensen Huang Seems Legitimately Confused In Confusing Ways</h4>


<p>This interview made me much more sympathetic to Jensen Huang.</p>
<p>My previous working hypothesis was ‘Jensen Huang knows and is lying’ because he lies about things and also he had to know better.</p>
<p>Whereas my new hypothesis has to be ‘Jensen Huang lies about other things. But on safety and the pressure to race he is actually and genuinely confused. His engineering mindset and total obsession with product perfection make him perfect for Nvidia. But they also blind him to the existential risks and potential for superintelligence and also make him completely unable to notice things about the situation that ordinary humans find obvious.’</p>
<p>I don’t get how he can not see this, but it seems clear that he doesn’t?</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Pacing the Frontier Letter: ​To realize A.I.’s potential, industry, government and society at large may need the option to buy time to address emerging risks, develop security measures and strengthen oversight.</p>
<p>But each company — and country — is under intense competitive pressure not to unilaterally slow that acceleration.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang: First of all, where’d that come from?</p>
<p>Ezra Klein: The labs.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang: No, no, that last sentence. Nobody’s putting the pressure on them. The U.S. —<strong>&nbsp;</strong>listen, there are 400 million Americans here. I believe that if everybody were just to take a vote, just right now, let’s just do this. If they need this, if that’s what they need, I’ll give them my vote.</p>
<p>Don’t ship the product. If your product is not ready to ship, don’t ship the product. This is the first time that I’ve heard a company or C.E.O. say that I need the laws, I need the antitrust laws to be relieved. I need the liability laws of products to be relieved so that I can pace myself.</p>
<p>That first paragraph is fantastic. I completely agree. Auditors, I completely agree. We have financial auditors. That’s great. Third-party safety auditors, financial auditors —<strong>&nbsp;</strong>that’s all great. That’s terrific.</p>
</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/j3xefrWrNqsmMfJEi/nxtd5ygrmurvvfc5zftj" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote>
<p>Ezra Klein: But you of all people, right? Nvidia is the fastest shipper around. For the history of your company, you were on a six-month product cycle ——</p>
<p>Jensen Huang: If our company is out of control, I promise you, we’ll close down.​</p>
<p>Ezra Klein: I believe you. I believe that you don’t run an out-of-control company.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang: Because the liabilities ——</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Ah, yes. The old-fashioned ‘if you release products that expose you to being sued that would be stupid, so obviously rapidly growing tech startup companies will definitely not be doing that.’</p>
<p>Done laughing yet?</p>
<p>Skipping ahead for a moment, Klein and Huang go back and forth many times, where Klein tries to point out incentives exist, and Huang keeps not getting this, in ways that strain credulity but I don’t get why you would do it as a bit.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Jensen Huang: ​Nobody is building more compute today than the people asking to be slowed down. It strikes me as odd.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>This is a classic game theory situation. This is labs recognizing their incentives are wrong. Also, they need all that compute anyway. Why is this hard?</p>
<p>(Jensen Huang clearly does not understand that ‘slow down’ is more a call to ‘not take a rocket ship straight to the moon quite yet’ rather than ‘net slow down from here.’)</p>


<h4>Solve Your Other Problems First and Get Back to Me</h4>


<blockquote>
<p>Ezra Klein: If you ship [persistent agentic systems] and it’s not ready, or even if you think it is ready and it’s not ready, then things could get very weird in our society, very fast.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang: Yeah. Hypothetically, you’re completely right, but all I’m suggesting is this: Before we go fix the hypothetical problems<strong>,&nbsp;</strong>before we go create more regulations, can we work on the practical problems that we know exist? Which is: We need to do a better job with containment and isolation; we should not allow a product to interact with the external world until it’s ready to be interacting with external worlds.​</p>
<p>I believe those two things are solvable problems. I believe they are solving it.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Things will definitely get weird if we do that quickly, even if the AI is ready, because society definitely will not be ready, and by weird I mean highly ungood things up to and including human extinction but definitely involving a lot of unfun disruptions, although it might end up working out.</p>
<p>That’s not Jensen’s central point here. His point here is more to get your own house in order before coming crying for help.
